\end{center}

\section{Exercises}\label{exercises-3}

The following exercises will help you practice the measures of
dispersion. In particular, the exercises work on:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Calculating the range, MAD, variance, and the standard deviation.
\item
  Using R to calculate measures of dispersion.
\item
  Calculating and using the Sharpe ratio to select investments.
\end{itemize}

Answers are provided below. Try not to peak until you have a formulated
your own answer and double checked your work for any mistakes.

\subsection*{Exercise 1}\label{exercise-1-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 1}

For the following exercises, make your calculations by hand and verify
results using R functions when possible. Make sure to calculate the
deviations from the mean.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Use the following observations to calculate the Range, MAD, Variance
  and Standard Deviation. Assume that the data below is the entire
  population.

  \begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccc@{}}
  \toprule\noalign{}
  \endhead
  \bottomrule\noalign{}
  \endlastfoot
  70 & 68 & 4 & 98 \\
  \end{longtable}
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The mean is} \(60\), the Range is \(94\), the MAD is \(28\), the
variance is \(1186\) and the variance is \(34.44\).

\emph{Here is the table of deviations:}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\(x_{i}\) & \(x_{i}-\bar{x}\) & \((x_{i}-\bar{x})^2\) &
\(|x_{i}-\bar{x}|\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
70 & 10 & 100 & 10 \\
68 & 8 & 64 & 8 \\
4 & -56 & 3136 & 56 \\
98 & 38 & 1444 & 38 \\
\end{longtable}

\emph{The variance averages out the squared deviations}
\((x_{i}-\bar{x})^2\), the MAD averages out the absolute deviations
\(|x_{i}-\bar{x}|\), and the standard deviation is the square root of
the variance.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\item
  Use the following observations to calculate the Range, MAD, Variance
  and Standard Deviation. Assume that the data below is a sample from
  the population.

  \begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccccc@{}}
  \toprule\noalign{}
  \endhead
  \bottomrule\noalign{}
  \endlastfoot
  -4 & 0 & -6 & 1 & -3 & 0 \\
  \end{longtable}
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The mean is \(-2\), Range is \(7\), the MAD is \(2.33\), the
variance is \(7.6\) and the standard deviation is \(2.76\).}

\emph{Here is the table of deviations from the mean:}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\(x_{i}\) & \(x_{i}-\bar{x}\) & \((x_{i}-\bar{x})^2\) &
\(|x_{i}-\bar{x}|\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
-4 & -2 & 4 & 2 \\
0 & 2 & 4 & 2 \\
-6 & -4 & 16 & 4 \\
1 & 3 & 9 & 3 \\
-3 & -1 & 1 & 1 \\
0 & 2 & 4 & 2 \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  Use the following observations to calculate the Range, MAD, Variance
  and Standard Deviation. Assume that the data below is a sample from
  the population.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2 & 8 & 4 & 10 & 6 & 6 \\
\end{longtable}

Answer

\emph{The mean is \(6\), Range is \(8\), the MAD is \(2\), the variance
is \(8\) and the standard deviation is \(2.83\).}

\emph{Here is the table of deviations from the mean:}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\(x_{i}\) & \(x_{i}-\bar{x}\) & \((x_{i}-\bar{x})^2\) &
\(|x_{i}-\bar{x}|\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2 & -4 & 16 & 4 \\
8 & 2 & 4 & 2 \\
4 & -2 & 4 & 2 \\
10 & 4 & 16 & 4 \\
6 & 0 & 0 & 0 \\
6 & 0 & 0 & 0 \\
\end{longtable}

\emph{The range is \(Range = 10 - 2 = 8\).}

\emph{The MAD is \(mad = (4+2+2+4+0+0)/6 = 12/6 = 2\).}

\emph{The sample variance is
\(s^2 = (16+4+4+16+0+0)/(6-1) = 40/5 = 8\).}

\emph{The standard deviation is \(s = \sqrt{8} = 2.83\).}

\subsection*{Exercise 2}\label{exercise-2-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 2}

You will need the \textbf{Stocks} data set to answer this question. You
can find this data here:

\href{stocks.xlsx}{\faIcon{file-excel} Download Data}

The data is a sample of daily stock prices for ticker symbols TSLA
(Tesla), VTI (S\&P 500) and GBTC (Bitcoin).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate the standard deviations for each stock. Which stock had the
  lowest standard deviation?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{For the sample taken, GBTC has the less variation. The standard
deviation of GBTC is \(9.43\), which is less than \(16.57\) for VTI or
\(50.38\) for TSLA.}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate the MAD. Does your answer in 1. remain the same?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The answer is the same, since the MAD for GBTC is \(8.46\) which
is lower than \(14.27\) for VTI or \(41.67\) for TSLA.}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  Finally, calculate the coefficient of variation. Any changes to your
  conclusions?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{By considering the magnitudes of the stock prices, it seems like
VTI is the less volatile stock. VTI has a CV of \(0.08\) which is lower
than \(0.44\) for GBTC or \(0.18\) for TSLA. In fact, by CV Bitcoin
seems to be the most risky asset.}

\subsection*{Exercise 3}\label{exercise-3-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 3}

You will need the \textbf{Portfolio} data set to answer this question.
The data has 100 records of the returns of two stocks. You can find the
data here:

\href{portfolio.xlsx}{\faIcon{file-excel} Download Data}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate the mean and standard deviation for each stock. Which
  investment has higher returns on average? Which investment is safest
  as measured by the standard deviation?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The best performing stock on average is stock X. It has an average
return of \(-0.078\)\% vs.~\(0.097\)\% for stock Y. The safest stock is
stock X as well, since the standard deviation is \(1.062\) percentage
points vs.~\(1.14\) percentage points for stock Y.}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Use a Risk Free rate of return of 3.5\% to calculate the Sharpe ratio
  for each stock. Which stock would you recommend?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

*The Sharpe Ratio measures the excess return per unit of risk taken.
Stock X has the better Sharpe Ratio \(-0.106\) vs.~\(-0.115\) for stock
Y. Stock X is recommended since it provides a higher excess return per
unit of risk taken.

\subsection*{Exercise 4}\label{exercise-4-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 4}

The monthly returns (in percent) for a small project can be found in the
Excel sheet below:

\href{monthly_returns.xlsx}{\faIcon{file-excel} Download Data}

Compute the sample mean, sample variance, sample standard deviation,
coefficient of variation (CV) (in percent), and the mean absolute
deviation (MAD). Briefly interpret the CV: what does it tell you about
the returns relative to their mean?

Answer

\emph{Start by computing the sample mean:}

\[\bar{x} = \frac{2.1 + (-0.5) + 3.0 + 1.2 + (-1.8) + 0.7 + 2.5 + (-0.3)}{8} = \frac{6.9}{8} = 0.8625\]

\emph{Here is the table of deviations from the mean:}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\(x_{i}\) & \(x_{i}-\bar{x}\) & \((x_{i}-\bar{x})^2\) &
\(|x_{i}-\bar{x}|\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2.1 & 1.2375 & 1.5314 & 1.2375 \\
-0.5 & -1.3625 & 1.8564 & 1.3625 \\
3.0 & 2.1375 & 4.5689 & 2.1375 \\
1.2 & 0.3375 & 0.1139 & 0.3375 \\
-1.8 & -2.6625 & 7.0889 & 2.6625 \\
0.7 & -0.1625 & 0.0264 & 0.1625 \\
2.5 & 1.6375 & 2.6814 & 1.6375 \\
-0.3 & -1.1625 & 1.3514 & 1.1625 \\
\end{longtable}

\emph{The sample variance is:}

\[s^2 = \frac{\sum(x_i - \bar{x})^2}{n-1} = \frac{19.2188}{7} = 2.7455\]

\emph{The sample standard deviation is:}

\[s = \sqrt{2.7455} = 1.657\]

\emph{The Mean Absolute Deviation is:}

\[mad = \frac{\sum|x_i - \bar{x}|}{n} = \frac{10.7}{8} = 1.3375\]

\emph{The Coefficient of Variation is:}

\[CV = \frac{s}{\bar{x}} \times 100 = \frac{1.657}{0.8625} \times 100 = 192.1\%\]

\emph{The CV is about \(192\%\), which is large: the standard deviation
is almost twice the mean. This indicates the returns are highly variable
relative to their average --- the mean is small compared with typical
month-to-month fluctuations. In practice, a CV this large signals that
the project's returns are unreliable; an investor cannot count on
earning close to the average return in any given month.}

\subsection*{Exercise 5}\label{exercise-5-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 5}

Prediction markets allow participants to bet on the outcomes of sporting
events. A bettor tracks his weekly profit and loss (in dollars) over ten
weeks of NFL betting in the spreadsheet below:

\href{nfl_betting.xlsx}{\faIcon{file-excel} Download Data}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Compute the sample mean, sample variance, sample standard deviation,
  and MAD. Is the bettor profitable on average?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The sample mean is:}

\[\bar{x} = \frac{120 + (-85) + 200 + (-150) + 95 + (-60) + 310 + (-220) + 175 + (-45)}{10} = \frac{340}{10} = 34\]

\emph{The bettor is profitable on average, earning \(\$34\) per week.}

\emph{Here is the table of deviations from the mean:}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\(x_{i}\) & \(x_{i}-\bar{x}\) & \((x_{i}-\bar{x})^2\) &
\(|x_{i}-\bar{x}|\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
120 & 86 & 7,396 & 86 \\
-85 & -119 & 14,161 & 119 \\
200 & 166 & 27,556 & 166 \\
-150 & -184 & 33,856 & 184 \\
95 & 61 & 3,721 & 61 \\
-60 & -94 & 8,836 & 94 \\
310 & 276 & 76,176 & 276 \\
-220 & -254 & 64,516 & 254 \\
175 & 141 & 19,881 & 141 \\
-45 & -79 & 6,241 & 79 \\
\end{longtable}

\emph{The sample variance is:}

\[s^2 = \frac{262{,}340}{9} = 29{,}149\]

\emph{The sample standard deviation is:}

\[s = \sqrt{29{,}149} = \$170.73\]

\emph{The MAD is:}

\[mad = \frac{86+119+166+184+61+94+276+254+141+79}{10} = \frac{1{,}460}{10} = \$146\]

\emph{The bettor is profitable on average but faces large week-to-week
swings --- a typical week deviates from the mean by about \(\$146\) to
\(\$171\) depending on the measure used.}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Compute the Coefficient of Variation (CV). What does it tell you about
  the reliability of the bettor's weekly results?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\[CV = \frac{170.73}{34} \times 100 = 502\%\]

\emph{A CV of \(502\%\) is extremely large (i.e., the standard deviation
is five) times the mean. This indicates that the average weekly profit
of \(\$34\) is tiny relative to the typical week-to-week swings of
\(\$171\). The bettor cannot reliably count on earning near the mean in
any given week. A high CV signals that the strategy is highly risky
relative to its average reward.}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  Assuming a risk-free weekly return of \$0, compute the Sharpe Ratio.
  Would you consider this a good risk-adjusted return?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{With a risk-free rate of \(\$0\):}

\[SR = \frac{\bar{x} - R_f}{s} = \frac{34 - 0}{170.73} = 0.199\]

\emph{A Sharpe ratio of \(0.199\) is low. As a point of reference, a
Sharpe ratio above \(1\) is generally considered acceptable in finance.
The bettor earns only \(\$0.20\) of profit per dollar of risk taken ---
suggesting that while the strategy is marginally profitable, the risk
taken to achieve that profit is not well compensated. A more disciplined
betting strategy or better odds selection would be needed to improve the
risk-adjusted return.}

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Descriptive Stats V}\label{descriptive-stats-v}

There are statistical measures that describe the shape and distribution
of the data beyond measures of central location or dispersion. They
provide a view of how data is spread out, where it concentrates, and how
it deviates from what might be expected. The tools shown below will help
you describe the data's shape, symmetry, and anomalies.

\section{Quantiles and Percentiles}\label{.quartiles}

A \textbf{quantile} is a location within a set of ranked numbers (or
distribution), below which a certain proportion, \(q\), of that set lie.
If we instead express quantiles as a percentage, they are referred to as
\textbf{percentiles}.

\textbf{Example:} Imagine all your data points lined up from smallest to
largest. If you say you're looking at the 25th percentile, it means
you're finding the value below which 25\% of your data falls. If you had
100 test scores, the 25th percentile would be the score where 25
students scored lower than that, and 75 scored higher or equal. It's a
way to see where a value stands in relation to the rest of the data in
terms of percentage.*

To calculate a percentile we follow the steps below:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Sort the data in ascending order. Each point has now a location. The
  smallest number will be first, the second smallest number will be
  second, etc.
\item
  Compute the location of the percentile desired using:
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Location of the pth percentile} \[L_{p}=1+k(n-1)\] where
\(L_{p}\)\$ is the location (in the sorted data) of the \(P_{th}\)
percentile, and \(P\) is the percentile desired.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  The data point at location \(L_p\), is the the \(P_{th}\) percentile.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Example:} Let's use the IQ scores for a group of students to
find the 25th percentile. \(IQ=\{80,100,110,75,130,90\}\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  We sort the data:
\end{enumerate}

\[IQ_{sorted}=\{75,80,90,100,110,130\}\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\item
  Find the location of the 25th percentile: \[L_{25}=1+0.25(6+1)=2.25\]
  The 25th percentile is in the position 2.25 of the sorted data.
\item
  Retrieve the 25th percentile: Since position 2 is 80 and position 3 is
  90, the 25th percentile lies \(0.25\) of the way between positions 2
  and 3. Hence, the 25th percentile is:
\end{enumerate}

\[P_{25} = 80 + 0.25(90 - 80) = 82.5\].

\section{Chebyshev's Theorem}\label{chebyshevs-theorem}

Chebyshev's Theorem is an important theorem, as it helps you form an
expectation of the proportion of data that must lie between a given
standard deviation from the mean. This offers a baseline to
understanding the range and distribution of your data, and aids in
detecting outliers. Formally, \textbf{Chebyshev's Theorem} states that
regardless of the shape of the distribution, at least (\(1-1/z^2\))\% of
the data lies between \(z\) standard deviations from the mean.

\textbf{CHEBYSHEV'S THEOREM} \[1 - \frac{1}{k^2}\] where \(k\) is the
number of standard deviations from the mean and \(k > 1\).

\textbf{Example 1:} For a given data set, we want to know at least how
much of the data lies within two standard deviations of the mean.
Substituting \(k=2\) into Chebyshev's formula yields
\(1-\frac{1}{2^2}=1-\frac{1}{4}=0.75\). Hence, at least \(75\%\) of the
data lies within two standard deviations from the mean.

\textbf{Example 2:} A financial analyst is reviewing the annual returns
of a mutual fund over several years. The mean annual return is \(8\%\)
with a standard deviation of \(3\%\). Using Chebyshev's theorem with
\(k=3\), at least \(1-1/9=0.889\) or \(88.9\%\) of the annual returns
lie within three standard deviations of the mean.

\section{The Empirical Rule}\label{the-empirical-rule}

Whereas Chebyshev's theorem holds for any data distribution, the
empirical rule is a bit more precise when looking at ``bell shaped''
data. Formally, the \textbf{Empirical Rule} or (\(68\),\(95\),\(99.7\)
rule) states that \(68\)\%, \(95\)\%, and \(99.7\)\% of the data lies
between \(1\), \(2\), and \(3\) standard deviations from the mean
respectively. The rule requires that the data be bell shape (normally)
distributed.

\textbf{Example:} A company reports that customer satisfaction scores
are are in good standing as they have a mean of \(\mu=78\) and a
standard deviation of \(\sigma=6\). They claim that \(95\%\) of
customers have satisfaction scores between \(66\) and \(90\). Note that
this conclusion is only valid if the data is truly bell-shaped --- a
histogram of the scores should be inspected to verify this assumption
before accepting the claim.

\section{Outliers (Z-Scores)}\label{outliers-z-scores}

Given the boundaries set by both the empirical rule and Chebyshev's
theorem, we can classify points as being common (normal) and not common
(outliers). Specifically, \textbf{outliers} are extreme deviations from
the mean. They are values that are not ``common'' or rarely occurring.
Since both the empirical rule and Chebyshev's theorem state that a large
proportion of the data is between three standard deviations, it would be
uncommon to have a data point that is more that three standard
deviations away from the mean.

To identify outliers we use a \textbf{z-score}, which is a measure of
distance from the mean in units of standard deviations. By definition,
\(z\)-scores above \(3\) are suspected to be outliers.

\textbf{THE Z-SCORE} \[z_{i}=\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s_x}\] where \(x_i\) is
the data point, \(\bar{x}\) is the sample mean, and \(s_x\) is the
sample standard deviation.

\textbf{Example:} On Jan 22, 2006 Kobe Bryant scored 81 points against
the Toronto Raptors. He had averaged 30 point per game with a standard
deviation of 4 points. If we calculate the z-score we get:*
\(z_{81}=\frac{81-30}{4}=12.5\). This mean that 81 is 12.5 standard
deviations away from the mean, making this value extremely rare.

\textbf{Example:} On March 10, 2026, Bam Adebayo of the Miami Heat
scored \(83\) points against the Washington Wizards --- the second
highest single-game scoring performance in NBA history, surpassing Kobe
Bryant's iconic \(81\)-point game. Prior to this game, Adebayo had
averaged approximately \(20\) points per game with a standard deviation
of \(5\) points. Calculating the z-score:

\[z_{83}=\frac{83-20}{5}=12.6\]

This means that Adebayo's \(83\)-point performance was \(12.6\) standard
deviations above his mean comparable in statistical terms to Kobe
Bryant's \(81\)-point game in 2006, which produced a z-score of
\(12.5\). Both performances illustrate how z-scores allow us to compare
exceptional events across different players and eras on a common scale.

\section{Skew}\label{skew}

A measurement of skew, identifies asymmetry in the distribution of data.
If most of the data leans towards one side, it's skewed. If it leans to
the left, it's left-skewed or negatively skewed, meaning the tail on the
left side is longer. If it leans to the right, it's right-skewed or
positively skewed, with a longer tail on the right. If the data is
evenly distributed, it's not skewed at all, it's symmetric. To determine
if the data is skewed, calculate the \textbf{Coefficient of Skew}.

\textbf{SKEWNESS}
\[Sk=\frac{n}{(n-1)(n-2)}\sum\left(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\right)^3\]
where \(n\) is the sample size, \(\bar{x}\) is the sample mean, and
\(s\) is the sample standard deviation.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\(Sk < 0\) & \(Sk = 0\) & \(Sk > 0\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Left-skewed & Symmetric & Right-skewed \\
\end{longtable}

The image below shows the different types of skew:

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{images/Skew.png}
\end{center}

The intuition behind the skewness formula lies in the cubed z-scores.
Recall that a z-score measures how far each observation is from the mean
in standard deviation units. Cubing these z-scores does two things: it
preserves the sign and it amplifies large deviations, giving outsized
influence to observations in the tails. If the right tail is longer, a
few large positive cubed z-scores dominate the sum, producing a positive
skewness. If the left tail is longer, large negative cubed z-scores
dominate, producing a negative result. A symmetric distribution produces
cubed z-scores that cancel out, yielding a skewness of zero.

\textbf{Example:} Consider the IQ scores
\(IQ=\{75, 80, 90, 100, 110, 130\}\) with \(n=6\), \(\bar{x}=97.5\), and
\(s=20.43\).

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.0959}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3562}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5479}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(x_i\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(\left(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\right)^3\)
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
75 & \(-1.101\) & \(-1.335\) \\
80 & \(-0.857\) & \(-0.628\) \\
90 & \(-0.367\) & \(-0.050\) \\
100 & \(0.122\) & \(0.002\) \\
110 & \(0.612\) & \(0.229\) \\
130 & \(1.591\) & \(4.024\) \\
\end{longtable}

\[Sk = \frac{6}{(5)(4)}\times(2.242) = \frac{6}{20}\times 2.242 = 0.673\]

Since \(Sk > 0\) the distribution is right-skewed, which makes intuitive
sense --- most IQ scores cluster below the mean while the score of
\(130\) pulls the tail to the right.

\section{Kurtosis}\label{kurtosis}

Kurtosis measures the ``tailedness'' of a distribution --- that is, how
much of the data is concentrated in the tails relative to a normal
distribution. A distribution with heavy tails has more extreme values
than expected, while a distribution with light tails has fewer. To
measure kurtosis, we use the \textbf{Excess Kurtosis} formula, which
centers the measure around zero by subtracting 3.

\textbf{EXCESS KURTOSIS}
\[K = \frac{n(n+1)}{(n-1)(n-2)(n-3)}\sum\left(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\right)^4 - \frac{3(n-1)^2}{(n-2)(n-3)}\]
where \(n\) is the sample size, \(\bar{x}\) is the sample mean, and
\(s\) is the sample standard deviation.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\(K < 0\) & \(K = 0\) & \(K > 0\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Platykurtic (light tails) & Mesokurtic & Leptokurtic (heavy tails) \\
\end{longtable}

Below you can see a graph illustrating the different types of kurtosis:

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{images/Kurtosis.png}
\end{center}

\textbf{Example:} Consider once more the IQ scores
\(IQ=\{75, 80, 90, 100, 110, 130\}\) with \(n=6\), \(\bar{x}=97.5\), and
\(s=20.43\).

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.0959}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3562}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5479}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(x_i\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(\left(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\right)^4\)
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
75 & \(-1.101\) & \(1.470\) \\
80 & \(-0.857\) & \(0.538\) \\
90 & \(-0.367\) & \(0.018\) \\
100 & \(0.122\) & \(0.000\) \\
110 & \(0.612\) & \(0.140\) \\
130 & \(1.591\) & \(6.401\) \\
\end{longtable}

Applying Excel's bias-corrected formula with \(n=6\):

\[K = \frac{6(7)}{(5)(4)(3)}(8.567) - \frac{3(5)^2}{(4)(3)} = 5.997 - 6.25 = -0.253\]

Since \(K < 0\) the distribution is platykurtic --- the tails are
lighter than a normal distribution.

A distribution is \textbf{leptokurtic} (\(K>0\)) when it has heavier
tails and a sharper peak than a normal distribution --- extreme values
are more common. A distribution is \textbf{platykurtic} (\(K<0\)) when
it has lighter tails and a flatter peak --- extreme values are rare.
When \(K=0\) the distribution has the same tail behavior as a normal
distribution and is called \textbf{mesokurtic}.

\textbf{Example:} A risk analyst examines the daily returns of two
assets. Asset A has an excess kurtosis of \(K=3.2\) and Asset B has an
excess kurtosis of \(K=-0.8\). Since \(K>0\), Asset A is leptokurtic ---
its returns have heavier tails than a normal distribution, meaning
extreme gains or losses occur more frequently than expected. Since
\(K<0\), Asset B is platykurtic --- extreme returns are rare and the
distribution is flatter. An investor concerned about tail risk would
prefer Asset B, as large unexpected losses are less likely.

\section{Five Point Summary}\label{five-point-summary}

A popular way to summarize data is by calculating the minimum, first
quartile, median, third quartile and maximum (five point summary). This
gives us a good idea of how data is distributed. We can additionally
inquire how the middle 50\% of the data varies. Recall, that we can use
a range to assess dispersion. The \textbf{interquartile range (IQR)}
quantifies the dispersion of the middle 50\% of the data. Formally, the
IQR is the difference between the third quartile (75th percentile) and
the first quartile (25th percentile).

\textbf{THE INTERQUARTILE RANGE (IQR)} \[IQR = Q_3 - Q_1\] where \(Q_1\)
is the first quartile (25th percentile) and \(Q_3\) is the third
quartile (75th percentile). The IQR measures the spread of the middle
\(50\%\) of the data.

\textbf{Example:} Let's use the IQ scores for a group of students once
more. Recall that the data is given by \(IQ=\{80,100,110,75,130,90\}\).
The minimum and the maximum are easily identified as \(Max=130\) and
\(Min=75\). The first quartile (\(P_{25}\)) was calculated in
\hyperref[.quartiles]{1.1} as 78.75. Using the same steps the third
quartile (\(P_{75}\)) is 115. The median is the average between the
third and fourth numbers \(Median=190/2=95\). The five point summary is
given in the table below:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ccccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\(Min\) & \(P_{25}\) & \(Median\) & \(P_{75}\) & \(Max\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
75 & 78.75 & 95 & 115 & 130 \\
\end{longtable}

To calculate the interquartile range we find the difference between the
75th and 25th percentiles. \(IQR=115-78.75=36.25\) which means that the
middle 50\% of the data has a range of 36.25.

\section{Outliers (IQR)}\label{outliers-iqr}

An alternate way to identify outliers is by using the interquartile
range. This measure and the z-score method can disagree. Any observation
falling outside the following limits is considered an outlier:

\textbf{OUTLIER LIMITS (IQR METHOD)}
\[Lower\ Limit = Q_1 - 1.5 \times IQR\]
\[Upper\ Limit = Q_3 + 1.5 \times IQR\] where \(Q_1\) is the first
quartile, \(Q_3\) is the third quartile, and \(IQR = Q_3 - Q_1\) is the
interquartile range.

If an observation \(x_i\) falls below the lower limit or above the upper
limit, it is considered an outlier.

\textbf{Example:} Consider once more the IQ data.
\[IQ=\{80,100,110,75,130,90\}\] The lower limit for on outlier is given
by: \[LL=Q_1-1.5(IQR)=78.75-1.5(36.25)=24.375\] The upper limit is given
by \[UL=Q_3+1.5(IQR)=115+1.5(36.25)=169.375\] Any data point outside the
range {[}24.375,169.375{]} is considered an outlier. In other words, 200
and 20 would be outliers, but 100 would not.

\section{Calculating Measures in
Excel}\label{calculating-measures-in-excel}

Excel provides functions to calculate all the shape and distribution
measures covered in this chapter. We will use the same \texttt{faithful}
dataset from R, which contains data on eruptions of the Old Faithful
geyser in Yellowstone National Park. The variable \texttt{eruptions}
measures the duration of each eruption in minutes.

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}cc}
\toprule
{\bfseries eruptions} & {\bfseries waiting} \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
3.600 & 79 \\ 
1.800 & 54 \\ 
3.333 & 74 \\ 
2.283 & 62 \\ 
4.533 & 85 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

Assuming the \texttt{eruptions} variable is in column A of your Excel
spreadsheet, the functions below will replicate the R output.

\subsection*{Outliers in Excel}\label{outliers-in-excel}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Outliers in Excel}

To identify outliers using the z-score method, calculate the z-score for
each observation using \texttt{=STANDARDIZE()}. In a new column, enter:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=STANDARDIZE(A2, AVERAGE(A:A), STDEV.S(A:A))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Copy the formula down for all observations. Any value greater than \(3\)
or less than \(-3\) in absolute value is a suspected outlier. To filter
outliers automatically, use \texttt{=IF()}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=IF(ABS(STANDARDIZE(A2, AVERAGE($A:$A), STDEV.S($A:$A))) \textgreater{} 3, "Outlier", "Normal")}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The z-scores for the first five observations of the \texttt{faithful}
dataset are:

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}ccc}
\toprule
{\bfseries eruptions} & {\bfseries waiting} & {\bfseries z\_eruptions} \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
3.600 & 79 & 0.4515 \\ 
1.800 & 54 & -1.2064 \\ 
3.333 & 74 & 0.2056 \\ 
2.283 & 62 & -0.7616 \\ 
4.533 & 85 & 1.3109 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

As confirmed earlier, none of the observations are outliers as all
z-scores are within three standard deviations of the mean.

\subsection*{Five Point Summary in
Excel}\label{five-point-summary-in-excel}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Five Point Summary in Excel}

To calculate the five point summary use \texttt{=QUARTILE.INC()}. The
argument \texttt{quart} takes values \(0\) through \(4\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 0)   /* Minimum    */}
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 1)   /* Q1 (P25)   */}
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 2)   /* Median     */}
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 3)   /* Q3 (P75)   */}
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 4)   /* Maximum    */}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The IQR can then be calculated as:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 3) {-} QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The five point summary for the \texttt{eruptions} variable is:

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}cccccc}
\toprule
{\bfseries Min} & {\bfseries Q1} & {\bfseries Median} & {\bfseries Q3} & {\bfseries Max} & {\bfseries IQR} \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
1.600 & 2.163 & 4.000 & 4.454 & 5.100 & 2.292 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

\subsection*{Skewness and Kurtosis in
Excel}\label{skewness-and-kurtosis-in-excel}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Skewness and Kurtosis in Excel}

To calculate skewness use \texttt{=SKEW()} and for excess kurtosis use
\texttt{=KURT()}. Both functions use the bias-corrected formulas
presented in this chapter:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=SKEW(A:A)    /* Skewness        */}
\NormalTok{=KURT(A:A)    /* Excess Kurtosis */}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The skewness and kurtosis for the \texttt{eruptions} variable are:

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}cc}
\toprule
{\bfseries Skewness} & {\bfseries Kurtosis} \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
-0.4135 & -1.5116 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

The negative skewness confirms the mild left skew identified earlier in
the chapter. The negative excess kurtosis indicates that the
distribution is platykurtic (i.e., the eruption durations have lighter
tails than a normal distribution), with fewer extreme values than
expected.

\section{Excel Function Summary}\label{excel-function-summary-2}

Below is a list of the Excel functions used in this section:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=PERCENTILE.INC(array,\ k)} returns the \(k\)th percentile of
  a range using the formula \(L_p = 1 + k(n-1)\), where \(k\) is
  expressed as a decimal between 0 and 1. This matches the method used
  in this chapter.
\item
  \texttt{=PERCENTILE.EXC(array,\ k)} returns the \(k\)th percentile
  using the formula \(L_p = k(n+1)\).
\item
  \texttt{=QUARTILE.INC(array,\ quart)} returns the five point summary.
  The argument \texttt{quart} takes values 0 (minimum), 1 (\(Q_1\)), 2
  (median), 3 (\(Q_3\)), and 4 (maximum).
\item
  \texttt{=STANDARDIZE(x,\ mean,\ std\_dev)} calculates the z-score for
  a value \texttt{x} given the mean and standard deviation.
  Alternatively, compute it manually as
  \texttt{=(A1\ -\ AVERAGE(A:A))\ /\ STDEV.S(A:A)}.
\item
  \texttt{=SKEW(range)} calculates the skewness of a range using the
  Fisher-Pearson standardized moment coefficient. Note that Excel does
  not have a direct equivalent to Pearson's Coefficient of Skew
  \(Sk=3(\bar{x}-Median)/s_x\) --- this must be calculated manually as
  \texttt{=3*(AVERAGE(range)\ -\ MEDIAN(range))\ /\ STDEV.S(range)}.
\item
  \texttt{=KURT(range)} calculates the excess kurtosis of a range. A
  positive value indicates leptokurtic (heavy tails) and a negative
  value indicates platykurtic (light tails).
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Summary Cheat
Sheet}\label{chapter-summary-cheat-sheet-2}

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{images/Other.png}
\end{center}

\section{Exercises}\label{exercises-4}

The following exercises will help you practice other statistical
measures. In particular, the exercises work on:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Constructing a five point summary and a boxplot.
\item
  Applying Chebyshev's Theorem.
\item
  Identifying skewness.
\item
  Identifying outliers.
\end{itemize}

Answers are provided below. Try not to peak until you have a formulated
your own answer and double checked your work for any mistakes.

\subsection*{Exercise 1}\label{exercise-1-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 1}

For the following exercises, make your calculations by hand and verify
results using R functions when possible.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Use the following observations to calculate the minimum, the first,
  second and third quartiles, and the maximum. Are there any outliers?
  Find the IQR to answer the question.

  \begin{longtable}[]{@{}ccccccc@{}}
  \toprule\noalign{}
  \endhead
  \bottomrule\noalign{}
  \endlastfoot
  3 & 10 & 4 & 1 & 0 & 30 & 6 \\
  \end{longtable}
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The minimum is} \(0\), the first quartile is \(2\), second
quartile is \(4\), third quartile is \(8\), and maximum is \(30\).
\(30\) is an outlier since it is beyond \(Q_{3}+1.5 \times IQR\).

\emph{Quartiles are calculated using the percentile formula}
\((n+1)P/100\). The data set has seven numbers. The first quartile's
location is \(8/4=2\), the second quartile's location is \(8/2=4\) and
the third quartile's location is \(24/4=6\). The values at these
location, when data is organized in ascending order, are \(1\), \(4\),
and \(10\).

\emph{In R we can get the five number summary by using the
\texttt{quantile()} function. Since there are various rules that can be
used to calculate percentiles, we specify type} \(6\) to match our
rules.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Ex1}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(Ex1,}\AttributeTok{type =} \DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
  0%  25%  50%  75% 100% 
   0    1    4   10   30 
\end{verbatim}

\emph{The interquartile range is needed to determine if there are any
outliers. The} \(IQR\) for this data set is \(Q_{3}-Q_{1}=9\). This
reveals that \(30\) is and outlier, since \(10+1.5 \times 9=23.5\).
Everything beyond \(23.5\) is an outlier.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Confirm your finding of an outlier by calculating the \(z\)-score. Is
  \(30\) an outlier when using a \(z\)-Score?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{If we use the} \(z\)-score instead we find that \(30\) is not an
outlier since the \(z\)-score is \(Z_{30}=2.15\). This observation is
only \(2.15\) standard deviations away from the mean.

\emph{In R we can make a quick calculation of the} \(z\)-Score to
confirm our results. The \(z\)-score is given by
\(Z_{i}=\frac{x_{30}-\mu}{\sigma}\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(Z30}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{30}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(Ex1))}\SpecialCharTok{/}\FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(Ex1))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 2.148711
\end{verbatim}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  Use Chebyshev's theorem to determine what percent of the data falls
  between the \(z\)-score found in \(2\).
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{Chebyshev's theorem states that} \(1-\frac{1}{z_{2}}\) of the data
lies between \(z\) standard deviation from the mean.

\emph{Substituting the} \(z\)-score found in 2. we get \(78.34\)\% of
the data lies between the standard deviation calculated. In R:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DecValTok{1{-}1}\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{(Z30)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 0.7834073
\end{verbatim}

\subsection*{Exercise 2}\label{exercise-2-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 2}

A financial analyst tracks the monthly revenue growth rates (in percent)
of a small e-commerce startup over five months:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ccccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2 & 2 & 2 & 6 & 13 \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate the sample mean and sample standard deviation.
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\[\bar{x} = \frac{2+2+2+6+13}{5} = \frac{25}{5} = 5\]

\[s = \sqrt{\frac{9+9+9+1+64}{4}} = \sqrt{23} = 4.796\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  For each observation compute the standardized value
  \(\frac{x_i - \bar{x}}{s}\), its cube, and its fourth power. Organize
  your results in a table.
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.0538}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1308}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3077}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3077}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(x_i\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(x_i - \bar{x}\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(\left(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\right)^3\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(\left(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\right)^4\)
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2 & \(-3\) & \(-0.625\) & \(-0.244\) & \(0.153\) \\
2 & \(-3\) & \(-0.625\) & \(-0.244\) & \(0.153\) \\
2 & \(-3\) & \(-0.625\) & \(-0.244\) & \(0.153\) \\
6 & \(1\) & \(0.208\) & \(0.009\) & \(0.002\) \\
13 & \(8\) & \(1.667\) & \(4.630\) & \(7.716\) \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  Use the results from part 2 to calculate the skewness. Is the
  distribution left-skewed, right-skewed, or symmetric?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\[Sk = \frac{5}{(4)(3)}\sum\left(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\right)^3 = \frac{5}{12}(-0.244-0.244-0.244+0.009+4.630)\]
\[= \frac{5}{12}(3.907) = 1.628\]

\emph{Since \(Sk > 0\) the distribution is right-skewed. Three months
had modest growth of \(2\%\) while one exceptional month of \(13\%\)
pulls the tail to the right.}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{3}
\tightlist
\item
  Use the results from part 2 to calculate the excess kurtosis. Is the
  distribution leptokurtic, mesokurtic, or platykurtic?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\[K = \frac{5(6)}{(4)(3)(2)}\sum\left(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\right)^4 - \frac{3(4)^2}{(3)(2)}\]
\[= \frac{30}{24}(0.153+0.153+0.153+0.002+7.716) - \frac{48}{6}\]
\[= \frac{30}{24}(8.177) - 8 = 10.221 - 8 = 2.221\]

\emph{Since \(K > 0\) the distribution is leptokurtic --- the tail is
heavier than a normal distribution, driven by the exceptional month of
\(13\%\) growth.}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{4}
\tightlist
\item
  Verify your results using \texttt{=SKEW()} and \texttt{=KURT()} in
  Excel.
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=SKEW(2,2,2,6,13)} → \(1.628\)
\item
  \texttt{=KURT(2,2,2,6,13)} → \(2.221\)
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Exercise 3}\label{exercise-3-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 3}

You will need the \textbf{Stocks} data set to answer this question. You
can find this data here:

\href{stocks.xlsx}{\faIcon{file-excel} Download Data}

The data is a sample of daily stock prices for ticker symbols TSLA
(Tesla), VTI (S\&P 500) and GBTC (Bitcoin).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}

  Construct a boxplot for Stock A. Is the data skewed or symmetric?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The data is skewed to the right.}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{DescriptiveV_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-11-1.pdf}}

\emph{The boxplot shows that there are no outliers. The data also looks
like it has a slight skew to the right.}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Create a histogram of the data. Include a vertical line for the mean
  and median. Explain how the mean and median indicates a skew in the
  data. Calculate the skewness statistic to confirm your result.
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The mean is more sensitive to outliers than the median. Hence,
when the data is skewed to the right we expect that the mean is larger
than the median.}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{DescriptiveV_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-12-1.pdf}}

\emph{The lines are close to each other but the mean is slightlty larger
than the median. Let's confirm with the skewness statistic}:

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 0.2856304
\end{verbatim}

\emph{This indicates that there is a slight skew to the right of the
data.}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  Use a line chart to plot your data. Can you explain why the data has a
  skew?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The line chart indicates that the data has a downward trend in the
early periods. This creates a few points that are large. In later
periods the stock price stabilizes to lower levels.}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{DescriptiveV_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-14-1.pdf}}

\subsection*{Exercise 4}\label{exercise-4-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 4}

You will need the \textbf{mtcars} data set to answer this question. The
dataset contains data on 32 automobile models and 11 variables including
miles per gallon (mpg), number of cylinders (cyl), horsepower (hp),
weight (wt), and transmission type (am), among others. You can find the
data here:

\href{mtcars.xlsx}{\faIcon{file-excel} Download Data}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Construct a boxplot for the \emph{hp} variable. Which car is the
  outlier?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The outlier is the Masserati Bora. The horse power is} \(335\).

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{DescriptiveV_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-15-1.pdf}}

\emph{From the graph it seems like the outlier is beyond a horsepower of
275, which is th Masserati Bora!}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Create a histogram of the data. Is the data skewed or leptokurtic?
  Calculate the skewness and kurtosis statistics to confirm your result.
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The histogram looks skewed to the right. This is confirmed by the
estimation of the coefficient of skewness of \(0.8\). The histogram also
shows also heavy tails which is confirmed with the kurtosis being

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{DescriptiveV_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-16-1.pdf}}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}

\tightlist
\item
  Transform the data by taking a natural logarithm. Specifically, create
  a new variable called \emph{Loghp}. Repeat the procedure in 2. Is the
  skew still there?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The skew is still there, but the distribution now looks more

\subsection*{Exercise 5}\label{exercise-5-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 5}

A quality control manager records the number of units produced per hour
by a manufacturing machine over eight shifts:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccccccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 7 & 8 \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
5 & 48 & 52 & 55 & 58 & 62 & 65 & 70 \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Find the five point summary: minimum, \(Q_1\), median, \(Q_3\), and
  maximum.
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{Sort the data: \(\{5, 48, 52, 55, 58, 62, 65, 70\}\).}

\emph{Using \(L_p = 1 + k(n-1)\):}

\emph{\(L_{25} = 1 + 0.25(7) = 2.75\) → \(Q_1 = 48 + 0.75(52-48) = 51\)}

\emph{\(L_{50} = 1 + 0.50(7) = 4.5\) →
\(Median = 55 + 0.5(58-55) = 56.5\)}

\emph{\(L_{75} = 1 + 0.75(7) = 6.25\) →
\(Q_3 = 62 + 0.25(65-62) = 62.75\)}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ccccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\(Min\) & \(Q_1\) & \(Median\) & \(Q_3\) & \(Max\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
5 & 51 & 56.5 & 62.75 & 70 \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate the IQR and the lower and upper outlier limits.
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\[IQR = Q_3 - Q_1 = 62.75 - 51 = 11.75\]

\[LL = Q_1 - 1.5 \times IQR = 51 - 1.5(11.75) = 51 - 17.625 = 33.375\]

\[UL = Q_3 + 1.5 \times IQR = 62.75 + 1.5(11.75) = 62.75 + 17.625 = 80.375\]

\emph{Any observation below \(33.375\) or above \(80.375\) is an
outlier. The value \(5\) falls below the lower limit and is therefore
identified as an outlier. This likely represents a shift where the
machine malfunctioned or was taken offline for maintenance.}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  Draw a box plot with whiskers and clearly mark any outliers.
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The box plot is constructed using the five point summary. The
whiskers extend from \(Q_1\) to the smallest non-outlier value (\(48\))
and from \(Q_3\) to the largest non-outlier value (\(70\)). The outlier
\(5\) is shown as a point below the lower whisker.}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{DescriptiveV_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-18-1.pdf}}

\emph{The box plot clearly shows the outlier at \(5\) units as an
isolated point well below the lower whisker. The remaining seven
observations are tightly clustered between \(48\) and \(70\), suggesting
the machine operates consistently when functioning normally.}

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Regression I}\label{regression-i}

Measures of association are essential tools in business statistics for
analyzing relationships between variables. They help determine whether
changes in one variable are linked to changes in another, providing
valuable insights into patterns and dependencies. Understanding these
relationships is critical for making informed decisions. By quantifying
the strength and direction of associations, businesses can better
interpret data, optimize strategies, and drive meaningful outcomes.
Below we study important measures of association.

\section{The Covariance}\label{the-covariance}

The \textbf{covariance} is a measure that determines the direction of
the relationship between two variables. It is calculated by:

\textbf{THE COVARIANCE}
\[s_{xy}=\frac{\sum(x_i-\bar{x})(y_i-\bar{y})}{n-1}\] where \(x_i\) and
\(y_i\) are observations, \(\bar{x}\) and \(\bar{y}\) are the sample
means, and \(n\) is the sample size.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\(s_{xy} > 0\) & \(s_{xy} = 0\) & \(s_{xy} < 0\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Direct relationship & No relationship & Inverse relationship \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Example:} Let's consider the following data that captures the
price of stocks (SPY) and bonds (BND):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Period & SPY & BND \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
1 & 87 & 316 \\
2 & 86 & 380 \\
3 & 84 & 416 \\
4 & 85 & 430 \\
5 & 83 & 440 \\
\end{longtable}

The idea behind calculating a covariance is to determine whether there
is a relationship between the two variables. We start by finding the
deviations from the mean for both SPY and BND:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Period & SPY & BND & \(SPY - \bar{SPY}\) & \(BND - \bar{BND}\) &
Product \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
1 & 87 & 316 & \(2\) & \(-80\) & \(-160\) \\
2 & 86 & 380 & \(1\) & \(-16\) & \(-16\) \\
3 & 84 & 416 & \(-1\) & \(20\) & \(-20\) \\
4 & 85 & 430 & \(0\) & \(34\) & \(0\) \\
5 & 83 & 440 & \(-2\) & \(44\) & \(-88\) \\
\end{longtable}

The covariance is the average of the products column using \(n-1\):

\[s_{xy} = \frac{-160 + (-16) + (-20) + 0 + (-88)}{5-1} =
\frac{-284}{4} = -71\]

Since \(s_{xy} < 0\) there is an inverse relationship between SPY and
BND. Intuitively, the covariance checks whether on average the products
of the deviations from the mean is positive or negative. Whenever \(x\)
lies below its mean and \(y\) lies above its mean, the product is
negative --- and vice versa --- reflecting the inverse relationship
between the two variables.

\section{The Correlation}\label{the-correlation}

The \textbf{correlation} measures the strength of the linear
relationship between two variables. It is calculated by:

\textbf{THE CORRELATION COEFFICIENT}
\[r = \frac{s_{xy}}{s_x \cdot s_y}\] where \(s_{xy}\) is the covariance,
\(s_x\) is the standard deviation of \(x\), and \(s_y\) is the standard
deviation of \(y\).

The correlation coefficient lies between \([-1, 1]\):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(r = -1\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(-1 < r < 0\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(r = 0\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(0 < r < 1\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
\(r = 1\)
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Perfect inverse & Inverse & No relationship & Direct & Perfect direct \\
\end{longtable}

The strength of the relationship is determined by the absolute value of
\(r\). Values close to \(1\) indicate a strong relationship, values
close to \(0\) indicate a weak relationship.

\textbf{Example:} Consider the SPY and BND data. The standard deviation
of SPY is \(s_{SPY} = 1.58\) and that of BND is \(s_{BND} = 50.37\).
Substituting into the correlation formula:

\[r = \frac{-71}{1.58 \times 50.37} = -0.89\]

This indicates that the relationship between SPY and BND is inverse and
strong.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The Coefficient of Determination
(\(R^2\))}{The Coefficient of Determination (R\^{}2)}}\label{the-coefficient-of-determination-r2}

The \textbf{coefficient of determination} or \(R^2\) measures the
percent of variation in \(y\) explained by variations in \(x\). It is
calculated by:

\textbf{THE COEFFICIENT OF DETERMINATION} \[R^2 = r^2\] where \(r\) is
the correlation coefficient. \(R^2\) ranges from \(0\) (no relationship)
to \(1\) (perfect relationship).

\textbf{Example:} Consider once more the SPY and BND example. The
correlation coefficient is \(r = -0.89\). Hence:

\[R^2 = (-0.89)^2 = 0.79\]

This indicates that about \(79\%\) of the variation in BND can be
explained by changes in SPY. The two variables are closely related.

\section{Measures of Association in
Excel}\label{measures-of-association-in-excel}

Excel provides straightforward functions to calculate all three measures
of association. Let's use the SPY and BND data from the example above.

\href{spybnd.xlsx}{\faIcon{file-excel} Download Data}

\textbf{Covariance:} To calculate the sample covariance use
\texttt{=COVARIANCE.S()}. Assuming SPY is in column A and BND is in
column B:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=COVARIANCE.S(A2:A6, B2:B6)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Correlation:} To calculate the correlation coefficient use
\texttt{=CORREL()}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=CORREL(A2:A6, B2:B6)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Coefficient of Determination:} There is no direct Excel function
for \(R^2\) between two variables. Simply square the correlation
coefficient:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=CORREL(A2:A6, B2:B6)\^{}2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The results for the SPY and BND data are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Covariance & Correlation & \(R^2\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(-71.00\) & \(-0.8916\) & \(0.79\) \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Scatter Plot:} To create a scatter plot in Excel, select both
columns and go to \texttt{Insert\ →\ Charts\ →\ Scatter}. This will
display the relationship between SPY and BND visually. To add a trend
line, right-click any point in the scatter plot and select
\texttt{Add\ Trendline\ →\ Linear}.

\section{Excel Function Summary}\label{excel-function-summary-3}

Below is a list of the Excel functions used in this section:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=COVARIANCE.S(array1,\ array2)} calculates the sample
  covariance between two variables. Use \texttt{=COVARIANCE.P()} for the
  population covariance.
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(array1,\ array2)} calculates the correlation
  coefficient between two variables. The result is always between \(-1\)
  and \(1\).
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(array1,\ array2)\^{}2} calculates the coefficient of
  determination \(R^2\) by squaring the correlation coefficient. Excel
  does not have a standalone \(R^2\) function for two variables.
\end{itemize}

\section{Exercises}\label{exercises-5}

The following exercises will help you understand statistical measures
that establish the relationship between two variables. In particular,
the exercises work on:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculating covariance and correlation.
\item
  Creating scatter diagrams in Excel.
\item
  Calculating the coefficient of determination.
\end{itemize}

Answers are provided below. Try not to peek until you have formulated
your own answer and double checked your work for any mistakes.

\subsection*{Exercise 1}\label{exercise-1-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 1}

For the following exercises, make your calculations by hand and verify
results using Excel functions when possible.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Consider the data below. Calculate the covariance and correlation
  coefficient by finding deviations from the mean. Verify your result
  using Excel. Is there a direct or inverse relationship between the two
  variables? How strong is the relationship?
\end{enumerate}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\textbf{x} & 20 & 21 & 15 & 18 & 25 \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{y} & 17 & 19 & 12 & 13 & 22 \\
\end{longtable}

Answer

\emph{The covariance is \(14.9\) and the correlation is \(0.96\). The
results indicate that there is a strong direct relationship between the
two variables.}

\emph{Start by finding the mean of each variable: \(\bar{x} = 19.8\) and
\(\bar{y} = 16.6\). Then build the deviations table:}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ccccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\(x_i\) & \(y_i\) & \(x_i-\bar{x}\) & \(y_i-\bar{y}\) & Product \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
20 & 17 & \(0.2\) & \(0.4\) & \(0.08\) \\
21 & 19 & \(1.2\) & \(2.4\) & \(2.88\) \\
15 & 12 & \(-4.8\) & \(-4.6\) & \(22.08\) \\
18 & 13 & \(-1.8\) & \(-3.6\) & \(6.48\) \\
25 & 22 & \(5.2\) & \(5.4\) & \(28.08\) \\
\end{longtable}

\[s_{xy} = \frac{0.08+2.88+22.08+6.48+28.08}{4} = \frac{59.6}{4} = 14.9\]

\[r = \frac{14.9}{sd(x) \times sd(y)} = \frac{14.9}{3.49 \times 4.34} = 0.96\]

\emph{To verify in Excel, enter x in column A and y in column B and
use:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=COVARIANCE.S(A2:A6,\ B2:B6)} → \(14.9\)
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A6,\ B2:B6)} → \(0.96\)
\end{itemize}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Consider the data below. Calculate the covariance and correlation
  coefficient by finding deviations from the mean. Verify your result
  using Excel. Is there a direct or inverse relationship between the two
  variables? How strong is the relationship?
\end{enumerate}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\textbf{w} & 19 & 16 & 14 & 11 & 18 \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{z} & 17 & 20 & 20 & 16 & 18 \\
\end{longtable}

Answer

\emph{The covariance is \(0.85\) and the correlation is \(0.148\). The
results indicate that there is a very weak direct relationship between
the two variables. They might be unrelated.}

\emph{To verify in Excel, enter w in column A and z in column B and
use:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=COVARIANCE.S(A2:A6,\ B2:B6)} → \(0.85\)
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A6,\ B2:B6)} → \(0.148\)
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Exercise 2}\label{exercise-2-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 2}

You will need the \textbf{mtcars} data set to answer this question. You
can download it here:

\href{mtcars.xlsx}{\faIcon{file-excel} Download Data}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate the correlation coefficient between \texttt{hp} and
  \texttt{mpg}. Is the relationship direct or inverse? How strong is it?
  Interpret the result.
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The correlation coefficient is \(-0.78\), indicating a moderately
strong inverse relationship between mpg and hp. As horsepower increases,
fuel efficiency decreases --- which makes intuitive sense.}

\emph{In Excel, assuming \texttt{mpg} is in column A and \texttt{hp} is
in column B:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A33,\ B2:B33)} → \(-0.78\)
\end{itemize}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Create a scatter diagram of \texttt{hp} (x-axis) and \texttt{mpg}
  (y-axis) in Excel. Add a linear trend line. Describe the pattern you
  observe.
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The scatter diagram is downward sloping: as horsepower increases,
miles per gallon tends to decrease. The points lie moderately close to
the trend line, which is consistent with the moderately strong inverse
correlation of \(-0.78\).}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate the coefficient of determination. What percentage of the
  variation in \texttt{mpg} is explained by \texttt{hp}?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The coefficient of determination is \(R^2 = (-0.78)^2 = 0.60\).
This means that \(60\%\) of the variation in miles per gallon is
explained by horsepower. The remaining \(40\%\) is explained by other
factors such as the car's weight, engine size, and fuel efficiency.}

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A33,\ B2:B33)\^{}2} → \(0.60\)
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Exercise 3}\label{exercise-3-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 3}

You will need the \textbf{College} data set to answer this question. You
can download it here:

\href{College.xlsx}{\faIcon{file-excel} Download Data}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Create a scatter diagram between \texttt{GRAD\_DEBT\_MDN} (Median
  Debt) on the y-axis and \texttt{MD\_EARN\_WNE\_P10} (Median Earnings)
  on the x-axis. Describe the relationship between the two variables.
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The scatter diagram shows a direct relationship: institutions
whose graduates have higher median earnings also tend to have higher
median debt. The upward pattern is moderate rather than tight, with a
fair amount of scatter around the trend line.}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate the correlation coefficient and the coefficient of
  determination between the two variables. Is the relationship direct or
  inverse? How strong is it? What percentage of the variation in
  earnings is explained by debt?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{The correlation coefficient is \(r = 0.46\), indicating a moderate
direct relationship between earnings and debt. The coefficient of
determination \(R^2 = 0.21\) indicates that only \(21\%\) of the
variation in earnings can be explained by debt. Other factors such as
field of study, institution type, and location likely explain the
remaining variation.}

\emph{In Excel, assuming \texttt{GRAD\_DEBT\_MDN} is in column A and
\texttt{MD\_EARN\_WNE\_P10} is in column B:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A1000,\ B2:B1000)} → \(0.46\)
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A1000,\ B2:B1000)\^{}2} → \(0.21\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{Note: use \texttt{=CORREL()} rather than \texttt{=COVARIANCE.S()}
when there are missing values, as CORREL handles them more gracefully in
Excel.}

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Regression II}\label{regression-ii}

Quantifying relationships between variables is an important skill in
business analytics. The regression line, representing the best linear
fit between two variables, enables businesses to make predictions,
identify trends, and optimize strategies using data-driven insights.
Understanding how to calculate and interpret a regression line provides
the foundation for analyzing business relationships, such as the effect
of advertising on sales or customer satisfaction on revenue. Below, we
delve into generating and analyzing a regression line.

\section{The Regression Line}\label{the-regression-line}

The regression line is calculated to minimize the average distance (or
errors) between the line and the observed data points. It is defined by
two key components: a \textbf{slope} (\(\hat{\beta}\)) and an
\textbf{intercept} (\(\hat{\alpha}\)). Mathematically, the regression
line is expressed as:

\textbf{THE REGRESSION LINE} \[\hat{y_i}=\hat{\alpha}+\hat{\beta}x_i\]
where \(\hat{y_i}\) are the predicted values of \(y\) given \(x_i\),
\(\hat{\alpha}\) is the intercept, and \(\hat{\beta}\) is the slope.

The \textbf{slope} determines the steepness of the line and quantifies
how much a unit increase in \(x\) changes \(y\) on average. It is
calculated using the covariance between \(y\) and \(x\) and the variance
of \(x\):

\textbf{THE SLOPE} \[\hat{\beta}= \frac{s_{xy}}{s_{x}^2}\] where
\(s_{xy}\) is the sample covariance between \(x\) and \(y\), and
\(s_x^2\) is the sample variance of \(x\).

The \textbf{intercept} determines where the line crosses the \(y\) axis
--- the predicted value of \(y\) when \(x\) is zero. Once the slope is
estimated, the intercept is calculated by:

\textbf{THE INTERCEPT} \[\hat{\alpha}=\bar{y}-\hat{\beta}\bar{x}\] where
\(\bar{y}\) is the mean of \(y\) and \(\bar{x}\) is the mean of \(x\).

\textbf{Example:} Let's examine a data set on Price and Advertisement.
In general, one expects that when a company advertises, it can convince
consumers to pay more for their product. Below is the data:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Advertisement (x) & Price (y) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2 & 7 \\
1 & 3 \\
3 & 8 \\
4 & 10 \\
\end{longtable}

The data shows a clear direct relationship between advertisement and
price. Regression allows us to answer two key questions:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Effectiveness:} How much can we increase the price for every
  additional dollar spent on advertisement?
\item
  \textbf{Prediction:} What is the predicted price if we have a budget
  of \(6\) for advertisement?
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Step 1 --- Calculate the slope:} Given that the covariance is
\(s_{xy}=3.67\) and the variance of \(x\) is \(s_x^2=1.67\):

\[\hat{\beta}= \frac{3.67}{1.67}=2.2\]

For every additional dollar spent on advertisement, price increases by
\(2.2\) on average.

\textbf{Step 2 --- Calculate the intercept:} Since \(\bar{x}=2.5\) and
\(\bar{y}=7\):

\[\hat{\alpha}=7-2.2(2.5)=1.5\]

If we do not advertise, the predicted price is \(1.5\).

\textbf{Step 3 --- Write the regression line:}

\[\hat{y_i}=1.5+2.2x_i\]

\textbf{Step 4 --- Make a prediction:} With an advertisement budget of
\(6\):

\[\hat{y_i}=1.5+2.2(6)=14.7\]

In conclusion, for every dollar spent on advertisement the price
increases by \(2.2\), and with a budget of \(6\) the predicted price is
\(14.7\).

\section{Measures of Goodness of Fit}\label{measures-of-goodness-of-fit}

When analyzing the effectiveness of a regression model, it is crucial to
assess how well the model fits the data. Below we revisit the
coefficient of determination \(R^2\) and introduce additional measures.

\subsection*{Coefficient of
Determination}\label{coefficient-of-determination}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Coefficient of Determination}

The \textbf{coefficient of determination} or \(R^2\) is the percent of
the variation in \(y\) that is explained by changes in \(x\). The higher
the \(R^2\) the better the explanatory power of the model.

\textbf{THE COEFFICIENT OF DETERMINATION} \[R^2=\frac{SSR}{SST}\]

where:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(SST = \sum(y_i-\bar{y})^2\) --- total variation in \(y\)
\item
  \(SSR = \sum(\hat{y_i}-\bar{y})^2\) --- variation explained by the
  model
\item
  \(SSE = \sum(y_i-\hat{y_i})^2\) --- variation unexplained by the model
\item
  \(SST = SSR + SSE\)
\end{itemize}

\(R^2\) is always between \([0,1]\).

\textbf{Example:} Consider data on the Weight (\(y\)) and Exercise
(\(x\)) of a particular person:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Weight (y) & Exercise (x) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
165 & 45 \\
170 & 10 \\
168 & 25 \\
164 & 30 \\
165 & 40 \\
\end{longtable}

If we used only the mean \(\bar{y}=166.4\) as our prediction, the total
squared error would be:

\[SST=(-1.4)^2+(3.6)^2+(1.6)^2+(-2.4)^2+(-1.4)^2=25.2\]

Using the regression line reduces these errors. The mistakes made by the
regression line are quantified by:

\[SSE=0.81+0.29+0.69+5.76+0.02=7.57\]

The improvement gained by using regression over the mean is:

\[SSR=5.29+9.4+0.59+0+2.35=17.63\]

Hence the coefficient of determination is:

\[R^2=\frac{SSR}{SST}=\frac{17.63}{25.2}\approx0.70\]

The regression line explains about \(70\%\) of the variation in Weight
using Exercise.

\section{Multiple Regression}\label{multiple-regression}

If we were to predict weight we could use several other variables to get
a better prediction. \textbf{Multiple regression} is a technique used to
predict a variable using more than one independent variable.

\textbf{THE MULTIPLE REGRESSION LINE}
\[\hat{y_i}=\hat{\beta_0}+\hat{\beta_1}x_1+\hat{\beta_2}x_2+...+\hat{\beta_k}x_k\]
where \(k\) is the total number of independent variables included in the
model.

\textbf{Example:} Let's consider an additional variable in our Weight
and Exercise example. The table below includes information on Calories
(\(z\)):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Weight (y) & Exercise (x) & Calories (z) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
165 & 45 & 1200 \\
170 & 10 & 1260 \\
168 & 25 & 1220 \\
164 & 30 & 1180 \\
165 & 40 & 1190 \\
\end{longtable}

The multiple regression line estimated by computer software is:

\[\hat{y_i}=83.96-0.025x+0.069z\]

Two conclusions follow from this result. First, as exercise increases
weight tends to go down, while more calories increases weight. Second,
reducing calorie consumption by \(1\) unit decreases weight by \(0.069\)
pounds, whereas one additional minute of exercise only reduces weight by
\(0.025\) pounds --- making calorie reduction more effective per unit.

\subsection*{Anova}\label{anova}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Anova}

The Anova table helps us understand how well each variable in our
regression model explains the dependent variable. It decomposes the
\(SSR\) by variable and tracks the remaining errors.

\textbf{Example:} For the Weight, Exercise, and Calories regression, the
Anova table is:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Source & Sum Squares \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
x & 17.63 \\
z & 6.55 \\
Residuals & 1.01 \\
\end{longtable}

Exercise explains \(17.63\) of the total variation. Adding Calories
reduces the remaining unexplained variation by another \(6.55\),
bringing the total \(SSR\) to \(24.18\) and the \(SSE\) down to
\(1.01\). The \(R^2\) increases from \(0.70\) to:

\[R^2=\frac{17.63+6.55}{25.2}=0.96\]

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{Adjusted
\(R^2\)}{Adjusted R\^{}2}}\label{adjusted-r2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Adjusted \(R^2\)}

The \textbf{adjusted} \(R^2\) penalizes a model for including additional
explanatory variables, preventing artificial inflation of \(R^2\).

\textbf{THE ADJUSTED \(R^2\)} \[\bar{R}^2=1-(1-R^2)\frac{n-1}{n-k-1}\]
where \(k\) is the number of explanatory variables and \(n\) is the
sample size.

\textbf{Example:} For the Weight example with both Exercise and Calories
(\(k=2\), \(n=5\), \(R^2=0.96\)):

\[\bar{R}^2=1-(1-0.96)\frac{5-1}{5-2-1}=0.92\]

\subsection*{Residual Standard Error}\label{residual-standard-error}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Residual Standard Error}

The \textbf{Residual Standard Error} estimates the average dispersion of
the data points around the regression line.

\textbf{THE RESIDUAL STANDARD ERROR} \[s_e=\sqrt{\frac{SSE}{n-k-1}}\]
where \(k\) is the number of independent variables and \(n\) is the
sample size.

\textbf{Example:} For the Weight and Exercise model (\(k=1\)):

\[s_e=\sqrt{\frac{7.57}{5-1-1}}=1.59\]

Adding Calories (\(k=2\)) reduces this to:

\[s_e=\sqrt{\frac{1.01}{5-2-1}}=0.71\]

The smaller residual standard error confirms that adding Calories
improves the model's fit.

\section{Regression in Excel}\label{regression-in-excel}

Excel provides a straightforward way to estimate regression models using
the \textbf{Data Analysis ToolPak}. If it is not already enabled, go to
\texttt{File\ →\ Options\ →\ Add-ins\ →\ Analysis\ ToolPak\ →\ Go} and
check the box.

\href{weight_exercise.xlsx}{\faIcon{file-excel} Download Data}

\textbf{Simple Regression (Weight and Exercise):}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Go to \texttt{Data\ →\ Data\ Analysis\ →\ Regression}
\item
  Set the \textbf{Input Y Range} to the Weight column
\item
  Set the \textbf{Input X Range} to the Exercise column
\item
  Check \textbf{Labels} if your data includes headers
\item
  Select an output location and click \texttt{OK}
\end{enumerate}

Excel will produce a full regression output including the coefficients,
\(R^2\), Adjusted \(R^2\), Standard Error, and Anova table --- all in
one step.

\textbf{Reading the Excel Output:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Intercept} row → \(\hat{\alpha}\)
\item
  \textbf{X Variable 1} row → \(\hat{\beta}\)
\item
  \textbf{R Square} → \(R^2\)
\item
  \textbf{Adjusted R Square} → \(\bar{R}^2\)
\item
  \textbf{Standard Error} → \(s_e\)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Multiple Regression (Weight, Exercise, and Calories):}

Follow the same steps but set the \textbf{Input X Range} to include both
the Exercise and Calories columns. Excel handles multiple predictors
automatically.

\textbf{Making Predictions:} Once you have the coefficients from the
output, use them directly in a formula. For the Advertisement and Price
example, if the intercept is in cell B17 and slope in B18:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=B17 + B18 * 6}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This returns the predicted price for an advertisement budget of \(6\).

\section{Excel Function Summary}\label{excel-function-summary-4}

Below is a list of the Excel functions and tools used in this section:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Data Analysis ToolPak → Regression} is the primary tool for
  estimating regression models in Excel. It returns coefficients,
  \(R^2\), adjusted \(R^2\), standard error, and the Anova table in a
  single output.
\item
  \texttt{=SLOPE(y\_range,\ x\_range)} calculates the slope
  \(\hat{\beta}\) of the simple regression line directly.
\item
  \texttt{=INTERCEPT(y\_range,\ x\_range)} calculates the intercept
  \(\hat{\alpha}\) of the simple regression line directly.
\item
  \texttt{=FORECAST.LINEAR(x,\ y\_range,\ x\_range)} returns the
  predicted value of \(y\) for a given value of \(x\) using simple
  linear regression.
\item
  \texttt{=RSQ(y\_range,\ x\_range)} returns the \(R^2\) for a simple
  regression.
\item
  \texttt{=STEYX(y\_range,\ x\_range)} returns the residual standard
  error \(s_e\) for a simple regression.
\end{itemize}

\section{Exercises}\label{exercises-6}

The following exercises will help you get practice on Regression Line
estimation and interpretation. In particular, the exercises work on:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Estimating the slope and intercept.
\item
  Calculating measures of goodness of fit.
\item
  Prediction using the regression line.
\end{itemize}

Answers are provided below. Try not to peak until you have formulated
your own answer and double checked your work for any mistakes.

\subsection*{Exercise 1}\label{exercise-1-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 1}

For the following exercises, make your calculations by hand and verify
results using R functions when possible.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Consider the data below. Calculate the deviations from the mean for
  each variable and use the results to estimate the regression line. Use
  R to verify your result. On average by how much does \emph{y} increase
  per unit increase of \emph{x}?
\end{enumerate}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\textbf{x} & 20 & 21 & 15 & 18 & 25 \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{y} & 17 & 19 & 12 & 13 & 22 \\
\end{longtable}

Answer

\emph{The regression lines is} \(\hat{y}=-4.93+1.09x\). For each unit
increase in x, y increases on average \(1.09\).

\emph{Start by generating the deviations from the mean for each
variable. For x the deviations are:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{x}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{21}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{15}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{18}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{25}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{(devx}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{x}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(x))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1]  0.2  1.2 -4.8 -1.8  5.2
\end{verbatim}

\emph{Next, find the deviations for y:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{y}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{17}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{22}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{(devy}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{y}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(y))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1]  0.4  2.4 -4.6 -3.6  5.4
\end{verbatim}

\emph{For the slope we need to find the deviation squared of the x's.
This can easily be done in R:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(devx2}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{devx}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1]  0.04  1.44 23.04  3.24 27.04
\end{verbatim}

\emph{The slope is calculated by}
\(\frac{\sum_{i=i}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})(y_{i}-\bar{y})}{\sum_{i=i}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^2}\).
In R we can just find the ratio between the summations of (devx)(devy)
and devx2.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(slope}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(devx}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{devy)}\SpecialCharTok{/}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(devx2))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 1.087591
\end{verbatim}

\emph{The intercept is given by} \(\bar{y}-\beta(\bar{x})\). In R we
find that the intercept is equal to:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(intercept}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(y)}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{slope}\SpecialCharTok{*}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(x))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] -4.934307
\end{verbatim}

\emph{Our results can be easily verified by using the \texttt{lm()} and
\texttt{coef()} functions in R.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{fitEx1}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{lm}\NormalTok{(y}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{x)}
\FunctionTok{coef}\NormalTok{(fitEx1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
(Intercept)           x 
  -4.934307    1.087591 
\end{verbatim}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate \emph{SST}, \emph{SSR}, and \emph{SSE}. Confirm your results
  in R. What is the \(R^2\)? What is the Standard Error estimate? Is the
  regression line a good fit for the data?
\end{enumerate}

Answer

\emph{SST is} \(69.2\), SSR is \(64.82\) and SSE is \(4.38\) (note that
\(SSR+SSE=SST\)). The \(R^2\) is just \(\frac{SSR}{SST}=0.94\) and the
Standard Error estimate is \(1.21\). They both indicate a great fit of
the regression line to the data.

\emph{Let's start by calculating the SST. This is just}
\(\sum{(y_{i}-\bar{y})^2}\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(SST}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{((y}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(y))}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 69.2
\end{verbatim}

\emph{Next, we can calculate SSR. This is calculated by the following
formula} \(\sum{(\hat{y_{i}}-\bar{y})^2}\). To obtain the predicted
values in R, we can use the output of the \texttt{lm()} function. Recall
